T4: bounded evidence re-review

16 sources reviewed · 53 staged records (52 active pending; 1 quarantined) · no new literature search. Existing approval states preserved. New records do not authorize executable suitability curves.

Decisions still needed

  • Ahmad 2018: existing canopy-cover record describes NDVI. Review replacement and retire incorrect mapping.
  • Haile: Table 3 and Table 5 weights disagree. No settled importance weight emitted. pH classes still conflict.
  • Nath: source slope stops at 44°; existing unbounded extension needs correction. Other class gaps/overlaps remain explicit.
  • Castle / Wotłolan / Mushtaq: species-selection or crop envelopes cannot automatically become general agroforestry rules.
  • WOCAT: observed slopes are context, not cutoffs; rootstock routing needs correction.
  • Ahmad 2020: six soil properties retain directions and nested weights in inventory, but lack safe canonical mappings. Categorical exclusions remain inventory-only pending exact claim-specific provenance.
  • Tea host mask remains Pete’s design decision; coffee/cocoa paper does not establish tea suitability.

New records: one review table

Direction tells how suitability changes. It does not establish numeric cutoffs. Source weights are reported importance within each paper, not default runtime weights.

The GIS model reclas-\nsiﬁes each criterion layer into a binary raster, then uses\nan add function to produce an intermediate layer with\nvalues ranging from 0 to n where n is the number of\ninput criteria (sum of each binary criterion). The\nmodel then creates a binary raster based on a\nconditional function where cells meeting all input\ncriteria (i.e. value = 6) are given a value of 1 and all\nother cells a value of 0. To identify whether the cells\nare within the Not suitable, Suitable (used), or\nSuitable (unused) land use class, the binary raster is\nmultiplied by the land use raster which has values of 0,\n1, or 2. From a multi-criteria decision analysis\nperspective this is an example of a conjunctive non-\ncompensatory aggregation MCDA method (Greene\net al. 2011; Jankowski 1995).\nThe combined crop optimum distribution model\n(Weighted Maximum) deals with the problem of\nspatial overlap in the cultivation criteria for the suite\nof target crops.       "quote": "interface provide judgements on the relative impor-\ntance of criteria to achieve a particular objective. The\ntool outputs the solution as a land suitability map with\ncategories of high, medium or low suitability (Modica\net al. 2016).\nIn this paper our aim is to present a set of methods\nthat use a combination of GIS and MCDA to evaluate\nland suitability for a selection of target crops for\nagroforestry spatial planning. The ﬁrst objective is to\nshow how commonly available edaphic and environ-\nmental cultivation criteria for agroforestry crops\n(through websites such as https://agroforestry.org/\nfree-publications/traditional-tree-proﬁles)\ncan\nbe\nused within a GIS to identify the most suitable areas\nfor target crops. Because suitable land areas for target\ncrops overlap, there is the issue of deciding which\nlocations are best suited to which crops. The second\nobjective is to present ways of dealing with the prob-\nlem of deciding where a suite of target crops should be\nplanted. We ﬁrst look at a method that deals with this\nproblem using an equitable distribution approach—\none that distributes the crops to locations based on the\nproportion of suitable land available to each crop. We\nthen examine a method that uses expert knowledge\nand MCDA to quantify the relative beneﬁts of each\ncrop and determines the spatial distribution of crops\nmost suitable for different agroforestry strategic goals.\nMaterials and methods\nStudy area\nThe Republic of Fiji is a tropical archipelago in the\nSouth Paciﬁc (Figs. 1). The most populated island,\nViti Levu, has approximately 600,000 inhabitants\nwhich is roughly 70% of the country’s population\n(CIA 2019). Viti Levu’s mountainous interior pro-\nduces an orographic effect making the west side of the\nisland much drier than the east (1800 mm annual\nrainfall vs. 3000 mm) (FMS 2011). One of the larger\nriver systems on the island is the Sigatoka River\ncatchment which starts in the Nausori highlands\n(approx. 1300 masl) and extends to the island’s\nsouthern Coral Coast. Sigatoka valley is often referred\nto as Fiji’s ‘‘salad bowl’’ because of its high soil\nfertility and high level of vegetable production. At the\nmouth of the river is the city of Sigatoka with a\npopulation of 58,940 (FBOS 2017). Along the Coral\nCoast are numerous tourist resorts, many of which take\nadvantage of market crops of the Sigatoka valley. Soil\nerosion due to persistent cultivation of market crops is\nresulting in soil losses of 24 to 80 tonnes per hectare\nper year (Wairiu 2016). Although agroforestry sys-\ntems are not common in the valley, there is signiﬁcant\npotential for their development. It is believed that the\nintroduction of multi-purpose trees through agro-\nforestry initiatives can diversify the livelihoods of\npeople, while rehabilitating degradation that has\noccurred as a result of current agricultural practices\n(Harrison and Karim 2016). While there are currently\nno active agroforestry initiatives in the valley, several\nmulti-purpose trees, such as lemon, Tahitian chestnut,\ncutnut, and breadfruit are grown in home gardens. The\nstudy area boundary for the work described in this\npaper corresponds to the Sigatoka catchment which is\napproximately 224,700 hectares.\nSelection of target crops\nAs a result of discussions with experts from Fiji\nMinistry of Agriculture and the Australian Centre for\nInternational Agricultural Research (ACIAR), seven\ntarget species were identiﬁed as potential crops for\nagroforestry initiatives in the Sigatoka valley. These\nwere: cutnut (Barringtonia procera), breadfruit (Ar-\ntocarpus altilus), lemon (Citrus limon), sandalwood\n(Santalum yasi), tropical almond (Terminalia cat-\nappa), Tahitian chestnut (Inocarpus fagifer), and vesi\n(Intsia bijua).\nCurrent land uses\nSpatial planning for agroforestry initiatives requires\ninformation about current land uses. Land uses such as\nnative forest, production forest, and settlements are\ngenerally incompatible with agroforestry cropping.\nOther land uses such as cultivated agricultural areas\nand pasture may be suitable but only on the margins\nand shelterbelts, and offer limited, but important\noptions for agroforestry. Bare land, grassland and\nother unused areas offer the highest potential for\nplanting agroforestry crops. For this project we\nreclassiﬁed a GIS dataset of current land uses in the\nstudy area into three classes: Not suitable (Forest,\nMangrove, Pine Plantation, Hardwood Plantation,\nShrubland,\nWater,\nUrban,\nSettlement),\n123\nAgroforest Syst (2021) 95:1519–1532\n1521\n",
